## Reasoning

**Analysis of Round 1 results:**

The champion (score 16884.88) uses:
- State 0: Exploration (rwm=24, slow turning) with transition to state 1 when perceiving color 3 (blue) with p=0.39
- State 1: ZigZag (zfr=62) with 4 transitions including neighbor-below conditions (few neighbors → back to explore), random probability, and gray floor detection

The mission is AGGREGATION - robots must gather in the blue-bordered strip (black floor). The champion's logic: explore until blue is perceived, then zigzag (which keeps robots near the blue wall area). The neighbor-below conditions in state 1 seem to send robots back to explore when isolated.

Wait - re-reading: the target is the BLUE-bordered strip. The floor in both strips is BLACK, gray in between. So robots need to be on BLACK floor in the BLUE strip. The robots can't distinguish which black strip they're in directly, but they can see blue walls (color 3 = blue? Let me check - clr uses {1,2,3,4,5,6}).

The champion uses color 3 (blue) detection to transition from exploration to zigzag. This makes sense: when a robot sees blue walls, it's near the target strip, so it switches to zigzag to stay there.

**Strategy:**
1. Keep the champion (elitism)
2. Exploit: small variations of the champion's parameters
3. Explore: different topologies using GoToColor(blue), attraction-based aggregation, etc.

For AGGREGATION in the blue strip:
- Robots need to find the blue strip (detect blue walls) and stay there
- GoToColor blue (B=8, clr=3) would actively move toward blue walls
- Once in the blue strip (black floor), stay with low-movement behavior
- Attraction to neighbors helps clustering

**Machines to generate:**

1. Champion (exact copy)
2. Champion variant: adjust p for blue detection (0.5 instead of 0.39)
3. Champion variant: adjust zfr (50 instead of 62)
4. Champion variant: different rwm (15 instead of 24)
5. 3-state: Explore → GoToColor(blue) when blue seen → ZigZag when few neighbors
6. 2-state: GoToColor(blue) → ZigZag on black floor
7. 3-state: Explore → ZigZag when blue seen → Attraction when many neighbors
8. 2-state: Exploration → Attraction when black floor detected
9. 3-state like champion but with GoToColor instead of ZigZag
10. 2-state: GoToColor(blue) with transition to stop on black floor + neighbor check
11. Champion variant: change neighbor thresholds
12. 4-state with more complex logic
13. 2-state: ZigZag → GoToColor(blue)
14. 3-state with FlockCenter

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key insight from Round 2:**

The champion (score 8873) is dramatically better than everything else. It uses:
- State 0: GoToColor(blue, clr=3) - actively moves toward blue walls
- Transition from state 0: if BLACK floor (c=0) with p=0.8 → stay in state 0; if neighbors ABOVE 3 (c=3, p=3, w=10) → go to state 1
- State 1: ZigZag (zfr=60) - once in the blue strip with enough neighbors, zigzag to stay
- Transition from state 1: if GRAY floor (c=1) with p=0.5 → back to state 0; if neighbors BELOW 2 (c=4, p=2, w=10) → back to state 0

The logic: robots go toward blue walls, transition to zigzag when they have 3+ neighbors (aggregated), and return to GoToColor if they drift to gray floor or lose neighbors.

There's a huge gap between rank 1 (8873) and rank 2 (13797), confirming this structure is very promising.

**Strategy:**
1. Keep champion exactly
2. Exploit: vary parameters of champion - thresholds, probabilities, zigzag frequency
3. Explore: add a 3rd state, try different neighbor thresholds, try attraction instead of zigzag, try stop behavior

Key parameters to vary:
- Black floor probability (0.8): try 0.6, 0.9, 0.95
- Neighbor threshold in state 0 (3, w=10): try 2, 4, 5
- ZigZag frequency (60): try 40, 80, 100
- Gray floor probability in state 1 (0.5): try 0.3, 0.7
- Neighbor-below threshold in state 1 (2, w=10): try 1, 3

Also explore: using Attraction instead of ZigZag in state 1, adding a 3rd state for Stop when fully aggregated.

## Reasoning

**Key insight from Round 3:**

The champion (score 4104.86) uses:
- State 0: GoToColor(blue) → if BLACK floor (p=0.8) stay; if neighbors ABOVE 3 (w=10) → go to state 1
- State 1: **Attraction** (att=3.0) → if GRAY floor (p=0.5) back to state 0; if neighbors BELOW 2 (w=10) back to state 0

The critical discovery: **Attraction** in state 1 is much better than ZigZag (4104 vs ~9000). This makes sense - once robots reach the blue strip, attracting to neighbors keeps them clustered together in the target area.

The 2nd place (4575) uses a 3-state machine with GoToColor→ZigZag+Attraction structure, also good but worse.

There's a big gap between rank 2 (4575) and rank 3 (7397), confirming the champion structure is excellent.

**Parameters to exploit around the champion:**
- Black floor probability (0.8): try 0.7, 0.85, 0.9
- Neighbor threshold (3, w=10): try 2, 4, 5; steepness 8, 12, 15
- Attraction gain (3.0): try 2.0, 4.0, 5.0
- Gray floor probability (0.5): try 0.3, 0.7
- Neighbor-below threshold (2, w=10): try 1, 3; steepness 8, 15

**Exploration:**
- 3-state: GoToColor → Attraction → Stop (when very many neighbors)
- 3-state: GoToColor → Attraction → GoToColor with different trigger
- Adding a 3rd transition to state 1 (e.g., perceive blue)
- Try FlockCenter instead of Attraction

## Reasoning

**Analysis of Round 5:**

The champion (3175.61) differs from the previous champion mainly in the neighbor threshold: p0x1=4 (instead of 3) with w=10. State 2 also uses p2x1=4. This means robots switch to Attraction when they have 4+ neighbors (more selective).

The top machines are all very close in score (3175-3606), suggesting we're near a local optimum. The big gap is between rank 11 (3606) and rank 12 (4471), and then a catastrophic jump to ranks 13-14 (~15000) which used FlockCenter/Attraction as state 2 instead of GoToColor.

**Key confirmed structure:**
- State 0: GoToColor(blue) + 3 transitions: BLACK floor→att, neighbors>4→att, random p=0.1→att
- State 1: Attraction(3.0) + 2 transitions: GRAY floor→GoToColor, few neighbors→GoToColor  
- State 2: GoToColor(blue) + 2 transitions: BLACK floor→att, neighbors>4→att

**Parameters to fine-tune:**
- Neighbor threshold in state 0/2: currently 4, try 3, 5
- Random escape probability: 0.1 → try 0.07, 0.12, 0.08
- Attraction gain: 3.0 → try 2.8, 3.2, 3.5
- Gray floor p in state 1: 0.5 → try 0.4, 0.6
- Neighbor-below in state 1: p=2, w=10 → try p=1, p=3, w=8, w=12
- Black floor p: 0.8 → try 0.75, 0.85

**Exploration:**
- Add 4th transition to state 0 (perceive blue color)
- Try p=5 neighbor threshold
- Mix neighbor thresholds between states 0 and 2
- Try att=3.5 with different gray p

## Reasoning

**Analysis of Round 9:**

The champion (2983.19) has: p0x0=0.75, neighbor>4 w=10, escape p=0.1, att=3, gray p=0.45, neighbor<2 **w=15** (steeper sigmoid for leaving attraction state). State 2 uses w=10 for neighbor threshold.

Ranks 2/3/4 are extremely close (2987.83 = same score, probably same seed average). The key finding: **w1x1=15** (steeper neighbor-below sigmoid) is better than w=10. This means robots leave the attraction state less readily when they have few neighbors.

Other observations:
- Rank 3 uses w0x1=15 (steeper entering attraction) combined with gray=0.45 - same score as rank 2
- Rank 4 uses att=3.5 - same score as rank 2
- Gray=0.42, 0.40, 0.35 are all slightly worse than 0.45
- Escape p=0.09 (rank 9) worse, p=0.1 confirmed good
- Neighbor threshold p0x1=3 (rank 12) clearly worse

The scores are converging. For the final round, I should:
1. Keep champion
2. Try w1x1=20 (maximum steepness for neighbor-below)
3. Combine w0x1=15 with w1x1=15
4. Try w2x1=15 (steeper in state 2 as well)
5. Try att=3.5 with w1x1=15
6. Try gray=0.45 with w1x1=20
7. Fine-tune around the champion with small perturbations
8. Try w0x1=15 AND w1x1=15 together
9. Try asymmetric: w1x1=15, w2x1=15

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

At every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is outside the target region. The score does not depend on how close the robots are to each other.

The arena has two strips at opposite ends. The floor is BLACK in both strips and GRAY in between. The target region is the strip whose bordering walls are BLUE; the walls bordering the other strip are GREEN.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 20 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.50 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=6  Spiral        -> traces an expanding spiral
            requires --spd<I>  integer in [1..100]  (spiral speed)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=10  FlockCenter   -> moves toward the centre of the group
            requires --fc<I>  integer in [1..100]  (flocking gain)
  B=11  ZigZag        -> moves in a zigzag pattern
            requires --zfr<I>  integer in [1..100]  (zigzag frequency)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
